=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a simple undirected graph with N vertices and M edges. The i-th edge connects vertices u_i and v_i bidirectionally.
Determine if there exists a way to write an integer between 1 and 2^{60} - 1, inclusive, on each vertex of this graph so that the following condition is satisfied:

- For every vertex v with a degree of at least 1, the total XOR of the numbers written on its adjacent vertices (excluding v itself) is 0.


What is XOR?

The XOR of two non-negative integers A and B, denoted as A \oplus B, is defined as follows:


- In the binary representation of A \oplus B, the bit at position 2^k \, (k \geq 0) is 1 if and only if exactly one of the bits at position 2^k in the binary representations of A and B is 1. Otherwise, it is 0.


For example, 3 \oplus 5 = 6 (in binary: 011 \oplus 101 = 110).

In general, the bitwise XOR of k integers p_1, \dots, p_k is defined as (\cdots ((p_1 \oplus p_2) \oplus p_3) \oplus \cdots \oplus p_k).  It can be proved that this is independent of the order of p_1, \dots, p_k.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
u_1 v_1
u_2 v_2
\vdots
u_M v_M

Output

If there is no way to write integers satisfying the condition, print No.
Otherwise, let X_v be the integer written on vertex v, and print your solution in the following format. If multiple solutions exist, any of them will be accepted.
Yes
X_1 X_2 \dots X_N

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 60
- 0 \leq M \leq N(N-1)/2
- 1 \leq u_i < v_i \leq N
- (u_i, v_i) \neq (u_j, v_j) for i \neq j.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 3
1 2
1 3
2 3

Sample Output 1

Yes
4 4 4

Other acceptable solutions include writing (2,2,2) or (3,3,3).

Sample Input 2

2 1
1 2

Sample Output 2

No

Sample Input 3

1 0

Sample Output 3

Yes
1

Any integer between 1 and 2^{60} - 1 can be written.

Sample Input 4

4 5
1 2
1 3
2 3
2 4
3 4

Sample Output 4

Yes
12 4 4 8

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

import sys

def solve():
    input=sys.stdin.readline
    N,M=map(int,input().split())
    adj=[[] for _ in range(N)]
    deg=[0]*N
    for _ in range(M):
        u,v=map(int,input().split()); u-=1; v-=1
        adj[u].append(v); adj[v].append(u)
        deg[u]+=1; deg[v]+=1
    rows=[]
    for v in range(N):
        if deg[v]>0:
            mask=0
            for to in adj[v]:
                mask |= 1<<to
            rows.append(mask)
    basis=[0]*N
    def add_row(x):
        while x:
            p=x.bit_length()-1
            if basis[p]:
                x ^= basis[p]
            else:
                basis[p]=x
                return
        # zero
    for row in rows:
        add_row(row)
    def in_span(mask):
        x=mask
        while x:
            p=x.bit_length()-1
            if basis[p]:
                x ^= basis[p]
            else:
                return False
        return True
    for v in range(N):
        if in_span(1<<v):
            print("No")
            return
    pivots=sorted([p for p,b in enumerate(basis) if b])
    pivot_set=set(pivots)
    kernel=[]
    for f in range(N):
        if f in pivot_set:
            continue
        y=1<<f
        # process pivots increasing
        for p in pivots:  # sorted increasing
            row=basis[p]
            # y currently has no bit p. But if p < f? Wait pivots sorted increasing, f is free. Could f be less than some pivots and greater than others. We process all pivots in increasing order. For a pivot p < f, y has free bit f set already; row_p may have bit f? Since row_p highest p < f, it cannot have bit f because f > p. So no issue. For pivot p > f, row_p may have bit f; y has f set. Good.
            if ((row & y).bit_count() & 1):
                y |= (1<<p)
        kernel.append(y)
    # If kernel empty? Then N=rank. But if no unit vector in R? Is that possible? If rank=N, row space is full GF(2)^N, so every unit vector in R, would have returned No. Since we didn't return, rank<N, kernel nonempty. But if N=0 not possible. If M=0, rank=0, kernel all unit vectors.
    labels=[0]*N
    for v in range(N):
        chosen=None
        for b in kernel:
            if (b>>v)&1:
                chosen=b
                break
        # chosen should not be None
        if chosen is None:
            # Should not happen; but maybe due to bug. Could compute solution directly? Let's think.
            print("No")? But condition says not forced zero. If chosen None, bug. Could fallback solve augmented. But maybe not needed. However, to be robust, we can implement a fallback to find y with y_v=1 using linear equations if kernel basis somehow lacks coordinate. But basis property guarantees. But if our kernel basis not a basis? It is. Still, maybe if coordinate v is not forced zero but our kernel basis vector for free f has bit v=1? Wait we argued any basis has coordinate v nonzero somewhere. Let's double-check: Suppose S = span{kernel_basis}. If for all b in kernel_basis, b_v=0, then any linear combination has v=0. So coordinate v identically zero. Contradiction. Thus chosen exists. Good.
        for u in range(N):
            if (chosen>>u)&1:
                labels[u] |= (1<<v)
    print("Yes")
    print(" ".join(str(x) for x in labels))

Yes, basis[q] has highest q and may contain lower bit p. But when reducing x, we process highest bit first. If x has highest bit p, then it has no bits >p. So no row with higher pivot q can help eliminate bit p without introducing bit q? Actually xoring basis[q] would introduce bit q (since basis[q] has highest q > p), which would increase x's highest bit, not helpful. In row-reduction for membership, we only eliminate highest bit using row with same leading bit. If no basis row with leading p, then x cannot be in span? Let's verify with non-reduced basis: Suppose basis row q contains bit p, and x = basis[q] + something? If x has highest p, but basis[q] has highest q > p, so any combination including basis[q] would have bit q unless canceled by another row with leading q. Since there is only one basis row with leading q (unique pivot), to cancel bit q you must include basis[q] an even number of times (0 or 2? over GF2, 2=0), so if x has no bit q, combination cannot include basis[q]. Thus only rows with leading <=p can be involved. To eliminate highest bit p, need a basis row with leading p. If none, not in span. So algorithm correct. Good.

Let's move to implementation details.

Potential issue: In kernel construction, we process pivots sorted increasing. But if pivot p's row contains a free variable f that is greater than p? Wait row_p has highest bit p, so it cannot contain any bit > p. Thus if f > p, row_p cannot contain f. So free variables greater than p don't affect y_p. That's correct because equation for pivot p only involves lower variables. If f < p, y has f set already. Good.

Potential issue: We set y initially to 1<<f. Then for each pivot p, if parity(row & y) is 1, set bit p. But row & y includes bits of lower pivots already set and free variables less than p. It does not include bit p because not set yet. But what if p was set as a lower pivot for an earlier pivot? We process p increasing, so p cannot have been set earlier because earlier pivots < p. So no. Good.

Potential issue: If pivot p's row contains another pivot q that is greater than p? impossible. Good.

Potential issue: If basis rows not reduced, row_p may contain a free variable f that is also a pivot? no, free variables are non-pivot. Good.

Potential issue: The kernel basis vector y for free f might have bit f=1, but if f is less than some pivot p, row_p may contain f and we set p accordingly. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
was cut off because it hit the maximum output length. The final code is below.
```python
import sys

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N, M = map(int, input().split())

    adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    deg = [0] * N
    for _ in range(M):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        u -= 1
        v -= 1
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1

    # Build the linear system:
    # For every vertex v with deg(v) > 0, XOR of labels of neighbors must be 0.
    rows = []
    for v in range(N):
        if deg[v] > 0:
            mask = 0
            for to in adj[v]:
                mask |= 1 << to
            rows.append(mask)

    # Gaussian elimination basis of the row space over GF(2).
    basis = [0] * N

    def add_row(x):
        while x:
            p = x.bit_length() - 1
            if basis[p]:
                x ^= basis[p]
            else:
                basis[p] = x
                return

    for r in rows:
        add_row(r)

    def in_span(mask):
        x = mask
        while x:
            p = x.bit_length() - 1
            if basis[p]:
                x ^= basis[p]
            else:
                return False
        return True

    # If e_v is in the row space, then every solution has x_v = 0.
    for v in range(N):
        if in_span(1 << v):
            print("No")
            return

    # Build a basis of the kernel (solution space).
    pivots = sorted(p for p, b in enumerate(basis) if b)
    pivot_set = set(pivots)

    kernel = []
    for f in range(N):
        if f in pivot_set:
            continue

        y = 1 << f
        for p in pivots:
            row = basis[p]
            if (row & y).bit_count() & 1:
                y |= 1 << p
        kernel.append(y)

    # Use one bit position for each vertex.
    labels = [0] * N

    for v in range(N):
        chosen = None
        for b in kernel:
            if (b >> v) & 1:
                chosen = b
                break

        # This must exist because e_v is not in the row space.
        if chosen is None:
            print("No")
            return

        for u in range(N):
            if (chosen >> u) & 1:
                labels[u] |= 1 << v

    print("Yes")
    print(" ".join(map(str, labels)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
